Let me think about the task. We need to prove in Rocq (Coq) that every closed term of type ∀X. X→X, when instantiated at U and applied to v of type U, can multi-step reduce to v (given nondeterminism, there exists a path).

This is a parametricity free theorem. The standard approach: define a logical relation indexed by types, with the twist that the relation must handle nondeterminism. Since the conclusion is "there exists a reduction path", the logical relation should relate terms to "candidates" perhaps, and we need the property that if t : ∀X. X→X, then t is related to some polymorphic identity-like value? Actually the free theorem: for any U, v : U value, app (tapp t U) v -->* v.

The standard semantic proof: logical relation R_U over closed terms and values. For a closed term of type ∀X.X→X, its relation at Ty_All says: for all U (well-formed?), for all v related to itself at U... Hmm.

The key challenge: defining the relation such that we can prove fundamental theorem, and then the desired conclusion.

Let me think: A standard "logical relation for nondeterminism" can be defined so that R_τ relates terms t and u if t reduces to a value related to u? Actually target: app (tapp t U) v -->* v. But perhaps simplest: define a logical relation R_ty : ty -> tm -> tm -> Prop such that:
- R_U t t' intuitively means t and t' both compute (multi-step) to related values, or something that gives us what we need.
- For base type variable X with an interpretation.

We want: t : ∀X.X→X closed. Then for U wf and v value with v:U, tapp t U -->* something applying identity to v.

The free theorem usually proved via relational parametricity: t is related to itself at ∀X.X→X, meaning for all types A, B related by R, and related arguments... Since we instantiate at U and v itself, we need identity relation on U values, but the term-level relation must imply the reduction path result.

The nondeterminism makes things subtle. Let's think about what relation makes the proof work and is provable.

### Framework design

We prove a general "fundamental theorem" in the style:

Define type environments:
- `TyEnv := list (ty * ty * (tm -> tm -> Prop))`? Actually for de Bruijn indices, variable 0 should be the innermost. When interpreting a type `T` with free metavariable indices, we want the relation for index i. But note that in the typing judgments, type variables in a context Δ are *free names* (atoms), while bound variables inside a Ty_All use de Bruijn indices. So we have two kinds of variables:
  - Ty_FVar X: free type variable from Δ.
  - Ty_BVar i: bound by enclosing Ty_All within the same type.

The typing rules open terms/types with fvar X for T_TAbs and T_Abs, so free variables are used. In the fundamental theorem, we can interpret contexts Δ with an environment mapping each atom X to a relation (and maybe a pair of types—but do we need the types for the relation? If our relation is diagonal (same type on both sides), no types needed! Let's explore diagonal).

Diagonal logical relation: R_T(t1,t2) for a single closed type T. Type variables: an atom environment ρ : list (atom * (tm -> tm -> Prop)) for free variables; BVar environment for bound variables. But wait, in the T_TAbs case, the typing derivation is under Δ' = X::Δ with body type open_ty T (fvar X), and IH gives R_{open_ty T (fvar X)} under some env containing X ↦ S. We then need to show R_{open_ty T A} of the substituted term/applied term. This needs a lemma: if R_{open_ty T (fvar X)}[X↦S] t t then R_{open_ty T A}[S applied to...] — but here's the subtlety: after substituting A for X in the type, the relation S doesn't appear; instead the relation at the type A position should be... Hmm. In diagonal parametricity, the relation instance S at the type variable is a relation on terms; after instantiating X := A, the relation used at occurrences of X is S (still a relation on terms "of type A"). The type A itself is not related to itself via S—rather, the terms of type A are related by S. So the "type assignment" for X is just S; no need to pair types. The term tapp (tabs t) A reduces to open_tm_ty t A. We need: R_{open_ty B A}[S for the binder] (open_tm_ty t A, ...).

The IH is about term `open_tm_ty t (fvar X)` and type `open_ty B (fvar X)` with free type var X interpreted by S. We need to convert this to the term `open_tm_ty t A` and type `open_ty B A`. The R relation is defined on type syntax where Ty_FVar X is looked up in the atom env and Ty_BVar i in the BVar env. Substituting A for X in the *syntax* gives a type where occurrences of X are replaced by A; the interpretation of A with the BVar env/semantic env is... not directly S; A is a closed type (say closed: wf [] A) and its interpretation is R_A. That's NOT S in general!

Wait, I think I'm confusing two levels. In relational parametricity, the universal quantifier is interpreted by quantifying over *relations between two types*, and then the body is interpreted with the type variable bound to that relation. If we do diagonal (single type), we'd quantify over a type A and a relation S on terms "of type A"? The body type B is interpreted by substituting A for X, and the type variable occurrence X is interpreted by S only if we treat the body's type as open with Ty_FVar X, not substituting. But then the term tapp t A's type is open_ty B A. To connect the two interpretations, we need a lemma that R_{open_ty B A} relates to R_{open_ty B (fvar X)}[X ↦ ?]. If at the syntactic type level we substitute A for X, the occurrence X becomes A, which is interpreted as R_A, not S. So this doesn't typecheck conceptually: we want the occurrence of X in B to be interpreted by S, not by R_A. Hence we must NOT substitute A into B when interpreting; instead interpret B in an environment mapping X ↦ (A, S)? But the relation R_A is not S. However, the *type* A appears in B after substitution, and the relation S is what we use for X. To make this coherent, the interpretation of B with X bound to S must be a relation between terms of type B[X:=A]; but our R_T is indexed by the syntax T, and the syntactic T we interpret is `open_ty B (fvar X)` with X free, not `open_ty B A`. The semantic type environment maps X to (A,S). Then when we need to relate the term of syntactic type `open_ty B A`, we must relate the interpretation of the syntactically different types. But actually we can just avoid substituting A syntactically: the term `open_tm_ty t A` has type `open_ty B A` by the typing rule, but for the logical relation we can consider the term as having type `open_ty B (fvar X)` in the *open* type environment X↦(A,S) even though it's "morally" the same. The logical relation definition is on type syntax and doesn't care about typing; we can interpret R at type syntax `open_ty B (fvar X)` with X ↦ (A,S) applied to the term `open_tm_ty t A`. We need a substitution/commutation lemma: R_{open_ty B (fvar X)}[X↦(A,S)] (tm_ty_subst X A' (open_tm_ty t (fvar X))?) Hmm.

This is getting complicated because the syntactic type has free X but the term no longer has free X. Let's define the semantics so that R for type syntax T under a type environment ρ (mapping Ty_FVar X to a pair (A, S), meaning "the type A with relation S") is defined on terms that are *substituted accordingly*. In other words, define R_T[ρ](t1,t2) where T is open syntax and the terms are expected to be in the substituted world. Then the definition:
- Ty_FVar X → S (the relation from ρ).
- Ty_BVar i → bound env relation.
- Arrow, All as usual.
- Crucially this definition doesn't depend on the term's free variables or types syntactically; R is just a relation on raw terms.
Then the fundamental theorem can state: if has_type Δ Γ t T, and we have environments: type env ρ assigning each X ∈ Δ a closed type A_X and relation S_X, and term env σ assigning each x : T_x in Γ related terms, then R_T[ρ](tσ1, tσ2)? But t itself is an open term; we need to substitute free type and term variables in t. So fundamental theorem would be: for all open terms t typed in Δ,Γ, and valuations substituting free type vars by closed types (per ρ) and free term vars by closed terms (per σ), R_T[ρ](t[X:=...][x:=...], ...). This requires substitution commutation lemmas for open_tm (the rules T_Abs/T_TAbs open binders, which interacts with the valuation substitution).

That's the standard "semantic typing" proof with the complication of locally nameless opening. It's substantial but doable.

Theorem fundamental :

```

Key challenge: nondeterminism requires the relation to handle terms that may reduce via choice. Let's design a relation that is *closed under multi-step reduction to related values on both sides* as above, and be careful to make the fundamental theorem provable.

Let me define:

```
Definition closed_rel (T : ty) (envB : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (envF : list (atom * (tm -> tm -> Prop))) : tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  fun t1 t2 => exists v1 v2, multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ value_rel T envB envF v1 v2.
```

where `value_rel` is defined recursively (structurally on T) as:
```
value_rel (BVar i) envB envF v1 v2 := nth_default (fun _ _ => False) i envB v1 v2
value_rel (FVar X) envB envF v1 v2 := lookup envF X default v1 v2
value_rel (Arrow A B) envB envF v1 v2 :=
   value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> value_rel A envB envF a1 a2 -> closed_rel B envB envF (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2)
value_rel (All B) envB envF v1 v2 :=
   value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall A1 A2 S, ... -> closed_rel B (S :: envB) envF (tm_tapp v1 A1) (tm_tapp v2 A2)
```

But `closed_rel` is defined using `value_rel`; and `value_rel` uses `closed_rel` at subterms. This is mutual recursion but with structural decrease on T from value_rel to closed_rel? closed_rel T calls value_rel T (same), value_rel Arrow calls closed_rel on A and B (subterms). So inlining: define closed_rel as a Fixpoint over T, where each branch computes value_rel inline. Then value_rel can be a Definition after closed_rel? But value_rel calls closed_rel at subterms, which are structurally smaller—if value_rel is defined by recursion on T and calls closed_rel (a Fixpoint) at subterms, that's fine if closed_rel is already defined... circular again.

We can define a single Fixpoint `Rel T` that includes both closed and value behavior:

```
Fixpoint Rel (T) envB envF (t1 t2) :=
  exists v1 v2, multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\
    match T with
    | BVar i => nthdef envB i v1 v2
    | FVar X => lookupdef envF X v1 v2
    | Arrow A B => value v1 /\ value v2 /\
        forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> Rel_v A envB envF a1 a2 -> Rel B envB envF (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2)
    | All B => value v1 /\ value v2 /\
        forall A1 A2 S, ... -> Rel B (S::envB) envF (tm_tapp v1 A1) (tm_tapp v2 A2)
    end
```

where `Rel_v A ...` is the "value relation" at A, which we can define as `fun a1 a2 => value a1 /\ value a2 /\ Rel A envB envF a1 a2`? Hmm, if we use Rel A and require a1,a2 values, then Rel A a1 a2 requires exists v1 v2, multi a1 v1... but since a1 is a value, multi a1 v1 with a1 value doesn't imply v1 = a1 (could reduce further? values are abs/tabs, which don't step? ST rules: abs and tabs are values; there are steps with abs/tabs on left? ST_AppAbs has tm_app (tm_abs ...) v, not the abs alone. ST_TAppTabs has tapp (tabs t) T. So abs and tabs alone don't step. But a value reached via multi then could step only in a context? `multi a1 v1` where a1 is a value: no step rule has a value as the whole left side except through app/tapp contexts. Since a1 is tm_abs or tm_tabs, no steps: multi value v implies v = value (by inversion on step). So requiring value a1 and Rel A a1 a2 with exists witnesses v1,v2... we can show v1 = a1, v2? Actually maybe define Rel in two layers with a well-founded mutual recursion using Coq's `Fixpoint` on T with a helper function? Let's try:

```
Fixpoint Rel (T : ty) (envB : list R) (envF : list (atom*R)) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  exists v1 v2, multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ valueRel T envB envF v1 v2
with valueRel (T : ty) (envB : list R) (envF : list (atom*R)) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | BVar i => nthdef i envB v1 v2
  | FVar X => lookupdef X envF v1 v2
  | Arrow A B => value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> valueRel A envB envF a1 a2 -> Rel B envB envF (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2)
  | All B => value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall A1 A2 S, lc_ty_at 0 A1 -> lc_ty_at 0 A2 -> Rel B (S::envB) envF (tm_tapp v1 A1) (tm_tapp v2 A2)
  end.
```

The mutual Fixpoint: Rel T calls valueRel T (same T), valueRel (Arrow A B) calls Rel B (smaller) and valueRel A (smaller), valueRel (All B) calls Rel B (smaller). So the mutual block has calls: Rel T -> valueRel T (same), valueRel T -> Rel sub and valueRel sub. Coq's guard condition for mutual fixpoints requires that recursive calls be on structurally smaller arguments? For mutual fixpoints, each function's recursive calls must be on a subterm of some argument of that function? Actually Coq's guard checker for nested fixpoints: In a mutual fixpoint, a call from f to g is allowed if some argument decreases, and calls within the block must have "principal argument" decrease. A call `valueRel T` from `Rel T` with same T is not decreasing—Coq may reject mutual recursion because of the non-decreasing call. Hmm. But we can inline valueRel into Rel to avoid the mutual block: define Rel so that at each type it has the form `exists v1 v2, multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ (match T with ... uses Rel on subterms ... end)`. That's a straightforward structural Fixpoint, because all Rel calls in the body are on subterms (A, B) except... the match branches: BVar/FVar use env; Arrow uses Rel A, Rel B; All uses Rel B. All subterms! Great — since valueRel's body is inlined and its recursive calls are Rel on immediate subterms. The only self-call at same T would be if the value relation at Arrow's application needed Rel B—which is a subterm. So a single Fixpoint works.

But we need to also handle the "value" constraint in the arrow clause: `forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> <valueRel A a1 a2> -> Rel B (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2)`. If we inline valueRel A (which is not Rel A but the match-form), we'd have to write it out. We can define valueRel as a separate Fixpoint *after* Rel? No—Rel's definition mentions valueRel A, and valueRel A mentions Rel B. If we inline valueRel A's formula into Rel's arrow branch, we get recursion Rel B on subterm, which is fine. - multi similar.
- open_ty/open_tm_ty respects same_shape.

Let's now think about whether the final theorem can be proven with this framework, and enumerate all needed lemmas:

### Definitions

- Relations env for bound type vars: `list (tm -> tm -> Prop)`.
- Type var env for free type vars: `list (atom * (tm -> tm -> Prop))` or a function `atom -> (tm->tm->Prop)`. A function is easier: `ty_env := atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop`. Iterating/extension: `fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then S else envF Y`. This avoids association list issues. Since atoms are nat, decidable.

But careful: free type variables in Δ that are not in envF? Fundamental theorem hypothesis Δ; we can require envF gives relations for all X in Δ. For T_TAbs, extend. For T_Var WF, no. Actually has_type uses Δ but types may mention any fvar? wf_ty premises constrain. We can just give relations for all atoms (a total function); when extending, override X. Then no side conditions. Good: `ty_rel_env := atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop`.

- `Sem T` returns a pair (Rel, VR) functions of envB, envF, terms. Since the type env isn't needed in Sem's construction except at BVar/FVar. Let's write:

```
Fixpoint Sem (T : ty) : (list Rel -> RelTyEnv -> tm -> tm -> Prop) * (list Rel -> RelTyEnv -> tm -> tm -> Prop) :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i =>
      let rel := fun envB envF t1 t2 =>
        exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ nth_rel i envB v1 v2 in
      let vr := fun envB envF v1 v2 => nth_rel i envB v1 v2 in
      (rel, vr)
  | Ty_FVar X =>
      let rel := fun envB envF t1 t2 => exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ envF X v1 v2 in
      let vr := fun envB envF v1 v2 => envF X v1 v2 in
      (rel, vr)
  | Ty_Arrow A B =>
      let relA := fst (Sem A) in let vrA := snd (Sem A) in
      let relB := fst (Sem B) in
      let vr := fun envB envF v1 v2 =>
         value v1 /\ value v2 /\
         forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> vrA envB envF a1 a2 -> relB envB envF (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2) in
      let rel := fun envB envF t1 t2 =>
         exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ vr envB envF v1 v2 in
      (rel, vr)
  | Ty_All B =>
      let relB := fst (Sem B) in
      let vr := fun envB envF v1 v2 =>
         value v1 /\ value v2 /\
         forall (A1 A2 : ty) (S : Rel), lc_ty_at 0 A1 -> lc_ty_at 0 A2 ->
           relB (S :: envB) envF (tm_tapp v1 A1) (tm_tapp v2 A2) in
      let rel := fun envB envF t1 t2 =>
         exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ vr envB envF v1 v2 in
      (rel, vr)
  end.
```

Note: `fst (Sem A)` in a Fixpoint: Coq accepts nested recursive calls in a Fixpoint if they're on subterms. The `fst (Sem A)` evaluates the recursive call to a pair; but the function's guard condition: recursive calls to Sem A with A a subterm of T, then applying fst/snd to the result — should be accepted since the recursive call is direct. However, note that Sem A is a function value; guard checker tracks that the recursive call's result is used. I think Coq allows matching on the result of a recursive call in a Fixpoint only if the recursive call is "the whole expression"? There's the "nested inductive" restriction: e.g., `Fixpoint f t := match f t with ... end` is rejected. Using `fst (Sem A)` where Sem is the recursive function may be rejected because the recursive call is not in a "guarded position"? Coq's guard condition allows recursive calls only in certain positions (arguments to constructors, functions, etc.). `fst (Sem A)` applies fst to a recursive call, which might be rejected? Let me test. A common workaround is to define a record-returning Fixpoint and destructure with a match in a Definition? The guard checker permits recursive calls as arguments to constructors and (I think) as function arguments? Actually the rule: recursive calls must appear in "guarded" positions; applying a function to a recursive call is not guarded? For structural recursion on a proof-irrelevant? Hmm, let's just test with Coq.

Alternatively, avoid returning pairs: define `Rel` and `VR` as separate Fixpoints using a mutual block where Rel calls VR at same T. Test if Coq accepts mutual recursion with same argument. I suspect it does NOT accept (guard condition requires strict decrease in mutual recursion). Let's test quickly.

Another clean workaround: define a single Fixpoint `RelAll T envB envF t1 t2` where the relation at all types is represented via a "tag" to select Rel vs VR? Or inline the value relation into the arrow branch by defining VR at subterms inline as `Rel A` (wrapped) but with values. Since values don't step, `Rel A v1 v2` with values equals the VR at A. Let's revisit: define only `Rel T env t1 t2` (wrapped) with the arrow clause:

```
Rel (Arrow A B) env t1 t2 :=
  exists v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧
  (∀ a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> Rel A env a1 a2 -> Rel B env (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2))
```

Because a1,a2 are values and Rel A a1 a2 with values equals the VR A a1 a2 (using no_step_value). Let's verify: Rel A a1 a2 with a1,a2 values = ∃ w1 w2, value w1 ∧ value w2 ∧ multi a1 w1 ∧ multi a2 w2 ∧ VR A w1 w2. Since a1 value and multi a1 w1, no_step_value gives a1 = w1 (need lemma: step v v' -> value v -> False? Actually values are abs/tabs; can `tm_abs T t --> t'`? The step relation has constructors with app/tapp/choice as left sides; abs/tabs are not left sides. So no step from values; prove by inversion: value v -> step v v' -> False). Then multi a1 w1 yields w1 = a1 (induction on multi with no_step). So Rel A a1 a2 = VR A a1 a2. Good, no need for a separate VR type family! The arrow clause can use `Rel A` with values and the value conditions, which matches the underlying value relation.

Similarly, the All clause:
```
Rel (All B) env t1 t2 :=
  exists v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧
  (∀ A1 A2 S, lc A1 -> lc A2 -> Rel B (S::env) (tm_tapp v1 A1) (tm_tapp v2 A2))
```
The universal's S is the raw relation for BVar 0. But the base case Rel (BVar 0) env' where env' = S :: env would be `∃ v1 v2 values, multi ... ∧ S v1 v2`. Hmm, the S here is raw, and Rel at BVar wraps with multis. The identity extension relates Rel (open_ty B U) env with Rel B (S::env) for which S? Let's recompute with this wrapped-only design. We need the T_TApp case: universal hypothesis gives Rel B (S::env) for all S. We want Rel (open_ty B U) env. Identity extension should be: Rel (open_ty B U) env t1 t2 ↔ Rel B (S_U :: env) t1 t2 where S_U := ??? We computed earlier with env entries "wrapped": if we set S := Rel U env (the wrapped relation), then RHS at BVar 0 is ∃ v1 v2 values, multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧ (Rel U env) v1 v2, which equals Rel U env t1 t2 (using a1 values no-step). So identity extension holds with S = Rel U env (wrapped). Good, no separate VR needed! But then in the T_TAbs case, the IH is at type open_ty B (fvar X) with envF X = ? and the goal is Rel B (S :: envB) where S is the universal's raw S. Identity extension would need S = Rel (fvar X) envF = wrapped envF X. So we should set envF such that the relation at fvar X, when wrapped, equals S. If we define envF X := S (raw), then Rel (fvar X) envF = ∃ v1 v2 values, multi ... ∧ S v1 v2, not S. Mismatch again, unless we choose the universal's S to be *wrapped* relations only: i.e., define the universal quantification over S but use S in the env directly; the identity extension for T_TAbs would require S = Rel (fvar X) under the IH's env, which is wrapped envF X. So if we set envF X := S (raw), then Rel (fvar X) = wrap S, and the identity extension would give Rel B (wrap S :: envB) instead of Rel B (S :: envB). But wait—the goal in the T_TAbs proof is Rel (All B) (tabs t), which unfolds to ∀ S, Rel B (S :: envB) ... for ALL S. Since S ranges over all relations, we could just take the universal S' = wrap S in the goal? But the goal is to prove it for all raw S; if the identity extension gives wrap S, we cannot conclude for raw S. However, note that Rel B (S :: env) and Rel B (wrap S :: env) might be equivalent? Not for arbitrary B: at B = BVar 0, Rel B with env head S' is wrap S' (the multis are already there), so Rel B (S :: env) = ∃ values multis ∧ S v1 v2. With S'=wrap S: ∃ v1 v2 multis ∧ (wrap S) v1 v2 = ∃ v1 v2 multis ∧ (∃ w1 w2 multis from v1... ∧ S w1 w2) = by no-step, ∃ v1 v2 multis ∧ S v1 v2. So equivalent! Because env entries are only ever applied to value witnesses (the Rel wrapper's witnesses are values). Both S and wrap S agree on values? S applied to values vs wrap S applied to values: wrap S v1 v2 (v1,v2 values) = ∃ w1 w2, value..., multi v1 w1, multi v2 w2, S w1 w2 = S v1 v2 by no-step. Yes! Since env relations are only applied to values (both in base Rel and in the identity extension), S and wrap S are indistinguishable. Nice.

So perhaps we don't need the identity extension to hold syntactically for raw S; we can prove a lemma with env entry `Rel U env` and, when needed, replace it with the raw S using an equivalence `Rel B (Rel U :: env) ≍ Rel B (wrap S :: env)`? Hmm, let's carefully redo the T_TAbs goal with the wrapped-only relation:

Goal: Rel (All B) envB envF (tabs t) (tabs t).
Unfold: ∃ v1 v2 values with multi (tabs t) v1 etc. Take v1=v2=tabs t (need value; no steps). Then need ∀ A1 A2 S, lc A1 -> lc A2 -> Rel B (S :: envB) envF (tapp (tabs t) A1) (tapp (tabs t) A2).

We reduce tapp (tabs t) A --> open_tm_ty t A, so by multi intro, it suffices to prove Rel B (S :: envB) envF (open_tm_ty t A1) (open_tm_ty t A2) for arbitrary closed A1,A2,S.

IH from T_TAbs: for all X fresh, has_type (X::Δ) Γ (open_tm_ty t (fvar X)) (open_ty B (fvar X)). Applying the fundamental theorem with envF' := envF[X := S] (raw S) and envB (same) gives:
  Rel (open_ty B (fvar X)) envB envF' (open_tm_ty (σ t) (fvar X)) (same).

Now we want to connect Rel (open_ty B (fvar X)) envB envF' to Rel B (S :: envB) envF. By the identity extension lemma in the form: for any BVar-free U,
  Rel (open_ty B U) envB envF' ≍ Rel B (Rel U envB envF' :: envB) envF' ... 
where Rel U envB envF' is the wrapped relation. For U = fvar X, Rel (fvar X) envB envF' = wrap (envF' X) = wrap S. So identity extension gives:
  Rel (open_ty B (fvar X)) envB envF' ≍ Rel B (wrap S :: envB) envF'.

And we need Rel B (S :: envB) envF. So we need the lemma that Rel B (S :: envB) envF ≍ Rel B (wrap S :: envB) envF for BVar-free? Alternative without projection issue: define Sem with an extra boolean/tag parameter? `Fixpoint Sem (tag : bool) (T : ty) : ... := match T with ...` where the recursive call is always `Sem tag' A` with tag computed; the guard checker cares about the decreasing argument T (a subterm) but the recursive call's tag argument may vary—that's fine? Guard checker usually requires the recursive argument to decrease; other arguments can be anything (as long as no less-than issues). But when the recursive call is under a match/projection? The result of Sem tag A is used directly as a function; no projection. This might work: define

```
Fixpoint SemRel (b : bool) (T : ty) : list Rel -> TyEnv -> tm -> tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | BVar i => fun envB envF t1 t2 =>
       if b then (exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ nth_rel i envB v1 v2)
       else nth_rel i envB t1 t2
  ...
  | Arrow A B => fun envB envF t1 t2 =>
       if b then (exists v1 v2, ... /\ (SemRel true T)?)
```

But inside, the arrow branch needs both the Rel and VR of A and B: `SemRel true A` and `SemRel false A`. Both are recursive calls on subterm A. That's fine! A single Fixpoint with a boolean flag. The recursive calls are `SemRel true A envB envF` etc., applied to envs and terms. The guard checker sees recursive calls on A, a subterm. Multiple recursive calls in the body are fine.

Then define:
- `RelT T envB envF t1 t2 := SemRel true T envB envF t1 t2`.
- `ValT T envB envF v1 v2 := SemRel false T envB envF v1 v2`.

But careful: when the flag is false, the semantic clauses should be the value relation, and recursive calls inside should use Rel at subterms:
- BVar false: nth_rel i envB t1 t2 (raw relation).
- Arrow false: value t1 /\ value t2 /\ ∀ a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> ValT A envB envF a1 a2 -> RelT B envB envF (app t1 a1) (app t2 a2).
- All false: value t1 /\ value t2 /\ ∀ A1 A2 S, lc A1 -> lc A2 -> RelT B (S::envB) envF (tapp t1 A1) (tapp t2 A2).
- BVar true: ∃ v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧ nth_rel i envB v1 v2.
- Arrow true: ∃ v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧ ValT (Arrow A B)?? Wait, we're inside the Arrow branch of T; we could just write the Val formula directly instead of calling SemRel false on T (same T, not a subterm!) ValT A ... -> RelT B ...). We can inline; the recursive calls are at subterms. Similarly All true inlines the Val formula with recursive call RelT B at subterm B. BVar/FVar true inline.
- FVar true/false similar.
- Arrow false: uses ValT A and RelT B at subterms.
- All false: uses RelT B at subterm B.

So the Fixpoint body's recursive calls are only at strict subterms; the boolean flag changes but that's fine. This should pass the guard checker. Let's test.

But there's a subtlety: in the Arrow false (Val) clause, we require `value a1 -> value a2 -> ValT A envB envF a1 a2 -> RelT B ...`. Then the true branch's value relation uses exactly this. Good.

Now the term-variable environment and substitution commutation. Let's define:

Term valuation: `tm_env := atom -> tm` for each side (σ1, σ2). Substitution function: `subst_tm_env σ t` replacing free fvars. We can define directly via a Fixpoint using a function `σ : atom -> tm`, with the same traversal as tm_subst. Or use tm_subst sequentially? A total function substitution is cleaner:

```
Fixpoint subst_tm (σ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => σ x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm σ t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm σ t1) (subst_tm σ t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (subst_tm σ t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (subst_tm σ t1) T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (subst_tm σ t1) (subst_tm σ t2)
  end.
```

Since it's a total function, no capture issues with de Bruijn binders. Standard.

Let's be more precise. We need a lemma that tracks the correspondence exactly. Let's define the inverse relation between lc_tm_at and open_tm_ty_rec:

Claim: `lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty_rec j U t)` iff `lc_tm_at (K+j) k t`? Maybe the right correspondence is: lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty_rec j U t) with U lc at ? corresponds to lc_tm_at (K+j)?? Let's think semantically. lc_tm_at K k t means: t has free type indices < K at top? At depth d under tabs, free indices allowed < K+d; and bound type indices are those ≥? Under d tabs, indices 0..d-1 are inner bound; the remaining free indices are shifted? No—de Bruijn indices in t at depth d: index i<d refers to inner binders; index i≥d refers to free variables, with original free index i-d? Similar to env discussion. lc_tm_at K k under d tabs allows i < K+d, so free indices i-d < K. So K is the number of free type variables allowed (in a de Bruijn *level* sense, indices ≥ d correspond to free level i-d < K? Hmm, where "free level" = index minus depth). So lc_tm_at K means free type levels < K.

open_tm_ty_rec j U t: at depth d under tabs, it replaces type index j+d with U. Wait, the parameter j is incremented under tm_tabs; at depth d, the replaced index is j+d. The free level of index j+d at depth d is j. So it replaces free type level j. Thus open_tm_ty_rec j U T replaces the free type variable with level j. If U is closed (lc_ty_at 0), then:
- t at type indices with free level < K: after replacing level j, the remaining free levels are... The opened term has free levels: levels < K except level j replaced by U; but indices > j? Since open_ty_rec doesn't decrement, index j+d replaced by U, and other indices unchanged. Free level of other indices: if level ≠ j, remains. So the opened term has free levels < K possibly minus j. If U is closed (level 0), then the opened term's free levels are those < K except j. Hmm, lc_tm_at K (opened) would allow free levels < K, but U has closed levels. So if t is lc at K (free levels < K), then opened is lc at K (since free levels remaining < K)? Let's check the direct direction: lc_tm_at K k t -> lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty_rec j U t) if lc_ty_at K U? At tm_tabs: t = tabs t1, lc_tm_at K k t needs lc_tm_at (S K) k t1. So forward: lc_tm_at K k t ∧ lc_ty_at K U -> lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty_rec j U t). Great, no shift.

Converse: lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_ty_rec j U t) ∧ lc_ty_at 0 U -> lc_tm_at (K?) k t. The free levels of t include level j (which got replaced), plus the free levels of opened (< K). Because in the tabs case, the opened term is lc_tm_at (S K) k (...), and IH with parameters (S K, S j) gives t1 lc at max(S K, S j + 1) = max(K+1, j+2). Target max(K,j+1)+1 = max(K+1, j+2). ✓. For tm_abs: opened lc at K gives lc_tm_at K (S k) (open_tm_ty_rec j U t1); IH gives t1 lc at max(K,j+1) (S k). We need the forward direction for: typing implies lc of terms in the *substitution* sense? Let's list lc needs:

Now the T_App case:
IH t1: RelT (Arrow T1 T2) [] envF (σ1 t1)(σ2 t1). Unfold: ∃ v1 v2 values, multi (σ1 t1) v1, multi (σ2 t1) v2, ValT (Arrow T1 T2) [] envF v1 v2.

  Similarly right.
  Composition of multis (transitivity).
Then Rel_multi_l twice gives the goal. ✓.

T_Choice: typing both branches T. IH1: RelT T [] envF (σ1 t1)(σ2 t1); IH2 similar. Goal RelT T [] envF (subst σ1 (choice t1 t2)) (subst σ2 (choice t1 t2)) = RelT T [] envF (choice (σ1 t1)(σ1 t2)) (choice (σ2 t1)(σ2 t2)).

Option A: Prove erasure invariance of RelT/ValT by induction on T. Need:
- `erase` function to an untyped term type, or just use a boolean relation `same_erase`.
- Prove step/multi transport: if same_erase t t' and multi t v then ∃ v', multi t' v' ∧ same_erase v v' ∧ value v'. Is this true given the step rules' lc side conditions? The lc conditions on annotations could differ between t and t' because annotations differ, but same_erase means annotations may differ. lc_ty_at on different types could be true for one and false for another! E.g., t = tm_abs (fvar X) body vs t' = tm_abs (BVar 0) body; same erasure, but the step ST_AppAbs on t requires lc_tm (tm_abs (fvar X) ...) which holds, while t' with BVar 0 annotation might not be lc, so t' may not step. In our use, the annotations are open_ty of the same underlying type with U1 vs U2, both locally closed if U1,U2 are lc (fvar X is lc too). So both terms are locally closed, and their step behavior should correspond. But erasure alone doesn't guarantee lc; we might need a relation that preserves lc.

Let's define a more refined annotation relation: `annot_related t t'` meaning same term structure (same erasure) and both locally closed? Or better, a function `ty_map` and prove step commutation. Hmm.

Alternative Option B: Avoid annotation irrelevance by strengthening the identity extension lemma to directly handle the terms: In the T_TAbs case, maybe instead of applying ST_TAppTabs and then needing annotations with A1, we can use the universal relation's quantification with S := ValT (fvar X)?? (fvar X), but the goal requires terms with A1? The goal's universal clause is specifically about tapp v1 A1 where A1 is the type argument. The only way to relate is through the step, which yields the A1-annotated opening. So no.

Option C: Make the typing rules' notion of types irrelevant by treating open_tm_ty as annotation-changing, and prove that the logical relation is invariant under *any* annotation change that preserves local closedness. Perhaps we can prove the relation is invariant under a type-substitution-style map that has the same "type skeleton". More concretely, define a relation `same_tm_shape` as erasure equality, and additionally assume both terms are locally closed? Then step transport might hold: if t and t' are lc, same erasure, and t --> u, is there u' with t' --> u' and same erasure u u'? Let's check the step rules:
- ST_AppAbs: t = app (abs T body) v with lc(abs T body), value v. t' same erasure: t' = app (abs T' body') v' with same erasures, and t' lc? The rule needs lc(abs T' body') — which holds if t' lc (inversion). value v'? same erasure and value v; does erasure-equality preserve value? value v means v = abs T v1 or tabs v1 with lc; v' same erasure with v' lc: if v is abs, v' same erasure is also abs (erasure preserves the constructor) with lc; so value v'. Then t' --> open_tm body' v', and erasure of open_tm body v vs open_tm body' v' should match (open/subst preserve erasure). ✓.
- ST_App1: app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2 with t1 --> t1' and lc t2. t' same erasure and lc: t' = app u1 u2 with same erasure subterms; u2 lc; by IH u1 --> u1' with same erasure; then app u1 u2 --> app u1' u2. ✓.

The resolution: The universal relation should quantify over relations S that are themselves defined on a type-erased representation, OR the logical relation at type variables should be applied to terms in a canonical annotation form.

Hmm wait, maybe the resolution is: The free theorem only needs the universal relation instantiated at types A1,A2 and relation S where S is a relation on values *of the actual instantiated type*. In relational parametricity with a *syntactic* type substitution, the terms at type B after substituting A have annotations A, and the relation S is meant to relate values "of type A"; there's no notion of annotation in the semantic model because types are part of the semantic interpretation. Our untyped syntactic relation is conflating annotations. To do it properly, we could define the logical relation on *terms annotated with the actual instantiation types*, i.e., perform the type substitution into terms as part of the semantics, and interpret types semantically (not syntactically). This is exactly the standard approach where types are interpreted as sets of (typed) terms and the universal quantifier quantifies over semantic relations between semantic types. The annotation issue disappears if we always use the substituted terms.

So maybe we should bite the bullet: In the fundamental theorem, substitute type variables syntactically in terms and types, and define the semantic relation on *substituted types*. But then how do we interpret the type variable occurrence X? The type scheme B is interpreted under a semantic environment; the actual type A is substituted into the term. The semantic relation at B under env maps to relation S at X, not Rel A. So the term, which now has annotations A, is related at the semantic type B[S]. The reduction behavior of the term's annotations (A) is what matters for steps; the relation doesn't otherwise inspect them. The annotation mismatch between the IH's open-with-X and the goal's open-with-A remains unless the IH is also stated with substituted terms.

Solution: state the fundamental theorem for an *arbitrary type substitution function* ρ_ty : atom -> ty (mapping free type vars to closed types, or to any types) applied to the term, and a *semantic relation environment* ρ_rel : atom -> Rel that is independent of the type substitution. The theorem: if has_type Δ Γ t T, then for any term env σ (pairs), RelSem T ρ_rel envB (subst_ty ρ_ty (subst_tm σ1 t)) ... where RelSem interprets T with ρ_rel for its free type vars and with BVar env. Then in T_TAbs, the body IH at type open_ty B X and with type substitution ρ_ty' = ρ_ty[X := ?]. The term is (open_tm_ty t X) with type substitution applied: subst_ty ρ_ty' (open_tm_ty t (fvar X)) = open_tm_ty (subst_ty ρ_ty t) (ρ_ty' X) if ρ_ty' is compatible. We need this to equal open_tm_ty (subst_ty ρ_ty t) A1, so set ρ_ty' X = A1. But the *semantic relation* at X should be S, and the semantic env for free type vars is ρ_rel; we set ρ_rel' = ρ_rel[X := S]. Since the relation and the type substitution are separate, RelSem (open_ty B X) with ρ_rel'? Hmm, but the type syntax open_ty B X has free X; RelSem under ρ_rel' X = S gives S at X. And identity extension: RelSem (open_ty B X) ρ_rel' envB = RelSem B (S::envB) ρ_rel'. Meanwhile the goal is RelSem B (S::envB) ρ_rel applied to terms with annotations substituted by A1. The type substitution in the term is A1, but the relation env is ρ_rel (not ρ_rel'). The difference between ρ_rel and ρ_rel' only matters for free type vars occurring in B other than X; since X is fresh in B, we can use envF extension. The terms: goal terms are open_tm_ty (subst_ty ρ_ty t) A1 and ...A2, while the IH terms would be subst_ty ρ_ty' (open_tm_ty t X) = open_tm_ty (subst_ty ρ_ty t) A1 (using ρ_ty' X = A1 and ρ_ty' = ρ_ty elsewhere, X fresh in t). So the terms match exactly! The left and right sides need different type substitutions: left uses A1, right uses A2. So the fundamental theorem must allow different type substitutions on the two sides: ρ_ty1 and ρ_ty2, with the semantic relation env a single ρ_rel that maps X to S. At X, the left term annotations use A1, right use A2, but the relation S is the same. That's exactly relational parametricity with a relation between different types.

So the fundamental theorem statement:

```
forall Δ Γ t T,
  has_type Δ Γ t T ->
  forall (relF : atom -> Rel) (sub1 sub2 : atom -> ty) (σ1 σ2 : atom -> tm),
    (forall X, In X Δ -> lc_ty_at 0 (sub1 X) /\ lc_ty_at 0 (sub2 X)?) ->
    term env condition for Γ under RelSem... ->
    RelSem T relF [] (ty_subst_tm sub1 (subst σ1 t)) (ty_subst_tm sub2 (subst σ2 t))
```

Hmm, but what are the type substitutions for free type variables? They map each free type variable X to an actual closed type (A1 or A2) with lc. The relation env relF maps X to the arbitrary relation S_X. There's no requirement linking sub and rel. Good.

Great. So the plan is solid but involves many lemmas:
1. Semantics: RelSem/ValSem as a single Fixpoint with a boolean flag, envB list, relF function.
2. Identity extension lemma (open_ty_rec / set_at) for both levels.
3. envB extension/independence lemmas.
4. envF extension lemma (free type vars).
5. Type substitution lemmas: ty_subst on types, tm_ty_subst on terms, commutations with open_ty, open_tm_ty.
6. Term substitution: subst_tm function, commutations with open_tm, open_tm_ty; FV agreement.
7. lc lemmas: wf_ty -> lc_ty_at 0; has_type -> locally_closed_tm; lc preservation under substitutions; opening inverse lemmas; type substitution lc.
8. multi lemmas: value no step, multi value, ST lifts, composition, multi under app/tapp/choice.
9. Rel-semantics lemmas: Rel_of_val, Rel_multi_l/r, ValT/RelT relation to multis, etc.
10. Fundamental theorem.
11. Final theorem.

Wait, let's revisit the diagonal universal relation idea with the fundamental theorem stated with type substitution. The problematic commutation is `tm_ty_subst sub' (tm_subst σ t) = tm_subst σ (tm_ty_subst sub t)`? No, the issue is the interaction at X. Let's write the exact terms:

In T_TAbs, the body typing is `has_type (X::Δ) Γ (open_tm_ty t (fvar X)) (open_ty B (fvar X))`. The fundamental theorem is applied with:
- type context Δ' = X::Δ, so the type substitution sub' must assign closed types to all variables in Δ' (including X): sub' X = A, sub' Y = sub Y for Y∈Δ.
- term substitutions σ1, σ2 (unchanged).
The IH conclusion: RelSem (open_ty B X) relF' [] 
   (ty_subst_tm sub' (subst σ1 (open_tm_ty t X)))
   (ty_subst_tm sub' (subst σ2 (open_tm_ty t X))).

We want: RelSem B (S::[]) relF [] (open_tm_ty (ty_subst_tm sub (subst σ1 t)) A) (similarly σ2).

So we need the term equality:
ty_subst_tm sub' (subst σ (open_tm_ty t X)) = open_tm_ty (ty_subst_tm sub (subst σ t)) A.

Let's expand `subst σ (open_tm_ty t X)`: open_tm_ty only changes annotations and inserts fvar X? Wait, open_tm_ty t (fvar X) inserts Ty_FVar X into type annotations, not term-level fvar X. The term-level substitution subst σ replaces term fvars by σ's terms. The order: subst after opening: subst σ (open_tm_ty t X) — term variables in t replaced by σ y. Those σ y terms may contain type annotations with fvar X. Then ty_subst_tm sub' applies sub' to all type annotations, including those inside the inserted σ y terms, mapping X↦A.

- `RelSem T envB envF t1 t2` (flag true? maybe I'll use flag false = term relation, true = value relation? The recursive calls: term relation at Arrow calls value relation at A (same T? no, A subterm) and term relation at B (subterm). Value relation at Arrow calls value relation A and term relation B. In a single Fixpoint with the flag, term-relation branch at Arrow inlines the value formula, which calls `Sem false A` and `Sem true B`. Value branch at Arrow calls `Sem false A` and `Sem true B`. So both branches have the same calls; the difference is just the wrapper and the inline formula. Let's write it with flag `true = term`, and the Arrow branch:

```
| Ty_Arrow A B => fun envB envF t1 t2 =>
    if mode then
      (exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\
        (value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> Sem false A envB envF a1 a2 -> Sem true B envB envF (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2)))
    else
      (value t1 /\ value t2 /\ forall a1 a2, value a1 -> value a2 -> Sem false A envB envF a1 a2 -> Sem true B envB envF (tm_app t1 a1) (tm_app t2 a2))
```

Could prove: for all t of type ∀X.X→X and all U,v as stated, t[U] v -->* v, by induction on t's typing derivation with a relation that "knows" U and v. The T_TAbs case still instantiates at U and needs the body at type open_ty B X with X := U. The annotation issue would arise in a syntactic proof too: the body typing after substituting? The typing rules don't have a type substitution rule, so to get from `has_type (X::Δ) ... (open_ty B X)` to `has_type Δ ... (open_ty B U)` one needs a type substitution lemma on typing derivations. That's standard and may be easier than the full parametric framework? We could prove a type substitution lemma for has_type, then do induction on the typing derivation of t at the *instantiated type*: e.g., prove a general theorem: for all closed terms t and closed types T, if has_type [] empty t T then ... some semantic property. The T_TAbs case: body has_type (X::[]) empty (open_tm_ty t0 X) (open_ty B X); apply type substitution X:=U to get has_type [] empty (open_tm_ty t0 U) (open_ty B U). Then use IH. But the IH for open terms (body) requires a semantic property at an open context X::[]; we'd still need the fundamental theorem with type contexts. This is essentially the same as the logical relation framework.

Maybe we can simplify by proving the fundamental theorem only for closed contexts but with type substitution: The typing derivation of the body is under Δ = [X]; type-substitute to [] and get a closed-context derivation, then apply IH. To do this recursively, the theorem should be: for all Δ, Γ, and terms... that's the fundamental theorem.

```
Fixpoint max_list (L : list nat) : nat :=
  match L with [] => 0 | x::xs => max x (max_list xs) end.
Definition fresh_atom L := S (max_list L).
Lemma max_list_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_list L. ...
Lemma fresh_atom_fresh : forall L, ~ In (fresh_atom L) L.
```

**Rel/Val definitions and basic lemmas**
- `RelSem` = Sem true, `ValSem` = Sem false.
- `RelSem_unfold`: for T constructors, cbn.
- `RelSem_multi_l`: RelSem T envB envF t1 t2 -> t1 -->* t1' -> RelSem T envB envF t1' t2? Hmm, actually if t1 reduces to t1' and RelSem t1' t2, then RelSem t1 t2. Let's define `RelSem_multi_l : RelSem T e f t1' t2 -> t1 -->* t1' -> RelSem T e f t1 t2` (prepend reductions on the left), and similarly right. Proof: unfold RelSem: ∃ v1 v2 values multis from t1'; compose with t1 -->* t1'. But the RelSem definition is uniform across T constructors: each true branch has the wrapper ∃ v1 v2, value... multi t1 v1 ... ∧ (base/val formula). We can prove a general lemma by induction on T? The true branch formula has the same wrapper shape for all T. We can prove:
```
Lemma RelSem_multi_l : forall T envB envF t1 t1' t2,
  (t1 -->* t1') -> RelSem T envB envF t1' t2 -> RelSem T envB envF t1 t2.
```
Proof: destruct T; cbn [RelSem Sem]; intros; destruct H0 as (v1 & v2 & hv1 & hv2 & m1 & m2 & Hrest); exists v1,v2; ... need to prepend multi. Use multi trans. For each constructor the wrapper shape may differ: BVar/FVar true branches are `exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ R`; Arrow true is `exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ (value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall...)`; All similar. So destruct works; use `multi_trans`. Need `multi_trans : multi a b -> multi b c -> multi a c`.
However, after destruct T and cbn, the `if mode then` needs mode true: RelSem is Sem true, so cbn should reduce the if. Good.
- Similarly RelSem_multi_r.
- `RelSem_of_val`: ValSem T envB envF v1 v2 -> value v1 -> value v2 -> RelSem T envB envF v1 v2. Proof: destruct T; cbn; for each true branch, take v1,v2 as witnesses, multi_refl, and the Val formula. Note the true branch's inner formula for Arrow is `value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall...` which should match ValSem's formula. cbn should make them syntactically identical. Let's check Arrow true: `exists v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧ (value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ ∀...)`. With t1=v1,t2=v2 and multi_refl, we need `value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ ∀...` which is exactly ValSem Arrow v1 v2. ✓. For All true: inner formula is `value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ ∀ A S ...` = ValSem All. ✓. For BVar true: inner `nth_rel i envB v1 v2` = ValSem BVar v1 v2 ✓. FVar ✓.

Where do we need ValSem implies values? In the arrow clause, ValSem Arrow includes values. In the All clause, includes values. **Identity extension**
Define `set_at_rel` as above. Lemma:
```
Lemma Sem_open_ty_gen : forall T k U envB envF,
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->
  (forall mode, Sem mode (open_ty_rec k U T) envB envF 
                = Sem mode T (set_at_rel k (ValSem U envB envF) envB) envF)? 
```
Hmm, equality of predicates (functions tm -> tm -> Prop) would require functional extensionality to prove `forall t1 t2, ... <-> ...`. Let's state pointwise iff:
```
Lemma RelSem_open_ty : forall T k U envB envF t1 t2,
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->
  (RelSem (open_ty_rec k U T) envB envF t1 t2 <-> RelSem T (set_at_rel k (ValSem U envB envF) envB) envF t1 t2)
```
and similarly ValSem. Prove both by induction on T (one lemma with a conjunction). For T=BVar i: open_ty_rec k U (BVar i) is if i=k then U else BVar i. cbn. Case i=k: LHS RelSem U envB = ∃ values multis ∧ ValSem U ... ; RHS RelSem (BVar i) (set_at_rel k V envB) where V=ValSem U envB = ∃ values multis ∧ nth_rel i (set_at k V envB) v1 v2. nth_rel i (set_at k V envB) = V when i=k. ✓ iff (both are ∃ values multis ∧ V v1 v2). Case i≠k: nth_rel i (set_at k V envB) = nth_rel i envB? set_at_rel k replaces position k without shifting; for i≠k, nth at i unchanged ✓ — need a lemma `nth_rel_set_at_ne`. Then both sides: LHS RelSem (BVar i) envB = wrap(nth i envB); RHS RelSem (BVar i) with env' = wrap(nth i env') = wrap(nth i envB) ✓ same without needing iff. But for ValSem: LHS = nth_rel i envB v1 v2 (if i≠k) or ValSem U envB v1 v2 (if i=k); RHS = nth_rel i env' = same ✓.
  Wait, if i=k: LHS ValSem (BVar i) envB = ValSem U envB = V; RHS ValSem (BVar i) (set_at k V envB) = nth_rel i (set_at k V envB) = V ✓.
  RelSem case i=k: LHS = wrap(V) and RHS = wrap(nth at k env' = V) ✓ identical.
  So BVar works.
- FVar: open_ty doesn't change; set_at doesn't matter; same envF ✓.

**Fundamental theorem**
Statement:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Δ Γ t T,
  has_type Δ Γ t T ->
  forall (sub : atom -> ty) (envF : TyEnv) (σ1 σ2 : atom -> tm),
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (sub X)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (σ1 x)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (σ2 x)) ->
    (forall x U, lookup_context x Γ = Some U -> RelSem U [] envF (σ1 x) (σ2 x)) ->
    RelSem T [] envF (subst_tm_env σ1 (subst_tm_ty_env sub t))
                      (subst_tm_env σ2 (subst_tm_ty_env sub t)).
```
Wait, the term formation: subst_tm_env σ (subst_tm_ty_env sub t). I need the order: subst_tm (subst_ty t)? Earlier I decided type-subst inside, term-subst outside: `tm_subst σ (tm_ty_subst sub t)` = subst_tm_env σ (subst_tm_ty_env sub t). ✓.

Proof by induction on the has_type derivation, generalizing sub, envF, σ1, σ2 and the hypotheses. Need to use `induction H` with H the typing derivation. The T_Abs/T_TAbs premises have forall over fresh x/X; the IH should be under the forall. Let's test the generated induction principle. We may need to use `induction H; intros; ...`. The tricky part: for T_Abs, the IH is for the body typing `has_type Δ (x|->T1;Γ) (open_tm t2 (fvar x)) T2` for each x∉L; we instantiate with a fresh x. Since the IH is under the forall in the constructor, Coq's induction principle likely gives `IH: forall x, ~ In x L -> (forall sub envF σ1 σ2, ... -> RelSem T2 ... (subst ... (open_tm t2 (fvar x))) ...)`. But our lemma's parameters (sub, envF, σ) are introduced after H in the statement; if we do `intros Δ Γ t T H` then `induction H`, the goal at each case has the other parameters universally quantified? Better: prove the theorem with all parameters before induction:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Δ Γ t T,
  has_type Δ Γ t T ->
  forall sub envF σ1 σ2, ... -> ...
```
When proving by `intros Δ Γ t T H; induction H`, the remaining forall sub envF... are in the goal for each case; we can `intros sub envF σ1 σ2 Hsub Hlc1 Hlc2 Henv`. But the IHs generated may or may not include the forall sub envF...; typically if the parameters appear after H in the goal, induction H will leave them forall in each case but the IH will be the property without them? Let's think: Coq's induction on H with goal `forall sub envF σ1 σ2, ... -> P`—actually the goal when doing induction H is `forall sub..., ...`, and the induction principle will instantiate the motive as the whole goal including the trailing foralls. The IH will then be `forall x, ~In x L -> (forall sub envF ..., ... -> P_body)`? I believe Coq generalizes the goal; the IH for a recursive occurrence will have the same trailing foralls. Usually it works: after `induction H`, the IH is something like `forall x H, ~ In x L -> forall sub ..., ...`. Let's test with a small example to be sure, or just try.

Actually, the standard issue: for T_Abs, the premise is `forall x, ~In x L -> has_type ...`. Coq's generated induction principle for has_type will have the IH as `forall x Hx, P x ...` where P is the motive (which includes the trailing foralls of our goal if we set the motive accordingly). Since we are proving a lemma with H as the last introduced assumption and the rest quantified, `induction H` will use a motive `fun Δ Γ t T H => forall sub envF σ1 σ2, ...`, and the IH will be under the forall x. Should be okay.

One complication: the IH for T_Abs is at the same Δ, context x|->T1;Γ, term open_tm t2 (fvar x), type T2. We need to instantiate the IH with the *same* sub' = sub and envF = envF? Yes, in T_Abs we use the same sub (diagonal), the same relF, and extended σ'. The IH's hypotheses: sub_ok ✓; env lc for σ' ✓; env relation condition for the extended context: for y in x|->T1;Γ:
  - y=x: lookup x (x|->T1;Γ) = Some T1 (since update prepends and Nat.eqb x x = true). Need RelSem T1 [] envF (σ1' x)(σ2' x) = RelSem T1 [] envF a1 a2. Have ValSem T1 [] envF a1 a2 + values → RelSem_of_val.
  - y≠x: lookup y (x|->T1;Γ) — if Nat.eqb y x = false, equals lookup y Γ; and σ'y = σ y. Need RelSem U [] envF (σ1 y)(σ2 y) from Henv y U. Theorem polymorphic_identity_theorem_for_free : forall t,
  has_type [] empty t (Ty_All (Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar 0))) ->
  forall U v, wf_ty [] U -> value v -> has_type [] empty v U ->

```
Definition RelTy := tm -> tm -> Prop.
Definition TyRelEnv := atom -> RelTy.
Definition BRelEnv := list RelTy.
```

Free variable functions:
```
Fixpoint ty_fv (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar _ => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => ty_fv T1 ++ ty_fv T2
  | Ty_All T1 => ty_fv T1
  end.

Fixpoint tm_fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs _ t1 => tm_fv t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_fv t1 ++ tm_fv t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_fv t1
  | tm_tapp t1 _ => tm_fv t1
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_fv t1 ++ tm_fv t2
  end.

Fixpoint tm_ty_fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar _ => []
  | tm_abs T t1 => ty_fv T ++ tm_ty_fv t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_ty_fv t1 ++ tm_ty_fv t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_ty_fv t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_ty_fv t1 ++ ty_fv T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_ty_fv t1 ++ tm_ty_fv t2
  end.

Fixpoint ctx_ty_fv (Γ : context) : list atom :=
  match Γ with
  | [] => []
  | (_,T) :: Γ' => ty_fv T ++ ctx_ty_fv Γ'
  end.
```

Note `context` = list (atom * ty) is defined earlier. Good.

Fresh:
```
Fixpoint max_list (l : list nat) : nat :=
  match l with [] => 0 | x :: l' => max x (max_list l') end.

Lemma max_list_ub : forall l x, In x l -> x <= max_list l.
Proof. induction l; simpl; intros; [contradiction|]. destruct H. subst; apply Nat.le_max_l. transitivity (max_list l); [apply IHl; auto | apply Nat.le_max_r]. Qed.
Definition fresh_atom (l : list atom) : atom := S (max_list l).
Lemma fresh_atom_notin : forall l, ~ In (fresh_atom l) l.
Proof. intros l H. unfold fresh_atom in H. apply max_list_ub in H. lia. Qed.
```

Substitution:
```
Fixpoint subst_ty_env (sub : atom -> ty) (T : ty) : ty :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => Ty_BVar i
  | Ty_FVar X => sub X
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => Ty_Arrow (subst_ty_env sub T1) (subst_ty_env sub T2)
  | Ty_All T1 => Ty_All (subst_ty_env sub T1)
  end.

Fixpoint subst_tm_ty_env (sub : atom -> ty) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs (subst_ty_env sub T) (subst_tm_ty_env sub t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm_ty_env sub t1) (subst_tm_ty_env sub t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (subst_tm_ty_env sub t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (subst_tm_ty_env sub t1) (subst_ty_env sub T)
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (subst_tm_ty_env sub t1) (subst_tm_ty_env sub t2)
  end.

Fixpoint subst_tm_env (σ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => σ x
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm_env σ t1)
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm_env σ t1) (subst_tm_env σ t2)
  | tm_tabs t1 => tm_tabs (subst_tm_env σ t1)
  | tm_tapp t1 T => tm_tapp (subst_tm_env σ t1) T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => tm_choice (subst_tm_env σ t1) (subst_tm_env σ t2)
  end.

Definition update_sub (sub : atom -> ty) (X : atom) (U : ty) : atom -> ty :=
  fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then U else sub Y.
Definition update_tyenv (envF : TyRelEnv) (X : atom) (R : RelTy) : TyRelEnv :=
  fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then R else envF Y.
Definition update_tmenv (σ : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (a : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb y x then a else σ y.
```

nth_rel and set_at_rel:
```
Definition nth_rel (i : nat) (env : BRelEnv) : RelTy :=
  match nth_error env i with Some R => R | None => fun _ _ => False end.

Definition set_at_rel (k : nat) (R : RelTy) (env : BRelEnv) : BRelEnv :=
  firstn k env ++ R :: skipn (S k) env.
```

Sem as tested.

Full proof:
```
Lemma lc_tm_open_tm_inv : forall K k j x t,
  lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_rec j (tm_fvar x) t) ->
  lc_tm_at K (Nat.max k (S j)) t.
Proof.
  intros K k j x t. revert K k j. induction t; intros K k j Hopened; cbn [open_tm_rec] in *.
  - (* bvar *)
    destruct (Nat.eqb j n) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst n. constructor. lia.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. constructor. inversion Hopened; lia.
  - (* fvar *) constructor.
  - (* abs *)
    inversion Hopened; subst.
    apply lc_tm_abs.
    + assumption.
    + replace (S (Nat.max k (S j))) with (Nat.max (S k) (S (S j))) by lia.
      apply IHt. assumption.
  - (* app *)
    inversion Hopened; subst.
    apply lc_tm_app.
    + apply IHt1. assumption.
    + apply IHt2. assumption.
  - (* tabs *)
    inversion Hopened; subst.
    apply lc_tm_tabs.
    replace (S (Nat.max k (S j))) with (Nat.max (S K) (S (S j))) by lia.
    apply IHt. assumption.
  - (* tapp *)
    inversion Hopened; subst.
    apply lc_tm_tapp.
    + apply IHt. assumption.
    + assumption.
  - (* choice *)
    inversion Hopened; subst.
    apply lc_tm_choice.
    + apply IHt1. assumption.
    + apply IHt2. assumption.
Qed.
```
Wait, for tabs: Hopened : lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_rec j x (tm_tabs t)). cbn in * might reduce open_tm_rec j x (tm_tabs t) to tm_tabs (open_tm_rec j x t)? Goal lc_tm_at K (max k (S j)) (tm_tabs t) → requires lc_tm_at (S K) (max k (S j)) t ✓. But my `replace (S (max k (S j))) ...` is wrong; it's `S K` that changes, not the k parameter. So for tabs:
```
    apply lc_tm_tabs.
    apply IHt. assumption.
```
No replace needed; IHt with K:=S K inferred from H's type? `apply IHt` needs to unify conclusion lc_tm_at (max K' (S j)) t with goal lc_tm_at (S K) (max k (S j)) t; unification sets K'=S K, leaving hypothesis lc_tm_at (S K) (open_tm_rec j x t) = H ✓ via assumption. Good.

For abs: Hopened : lc_tm_at K k (tm_abs T (open_tm_rec (S j) x t)); inversion gives lc_ty_at K T and lc_tm_at K (S k) (open_tm_rec (S j) x t). Goal lc_tm_at K (max k (S j)) (tm_abs T t) = requires lc_ty_at K T ✓ and lc_tm_at K (S (max k (S j))) t. For tapp: Hopened : lc_tm_at K k (tm_tapp (open_tm_rec j x t) T); inversion gives lc_tm_at K k (open_tm_rec j x t) and lc_ty_at K T. Goal tapp at max K (S j): term via IH, type same K? lc_ty_at K T; goal needs lc_ty_at (max K (S j)) T. We have lc_ty_at K T; use mono_le with K<=max. `apply lc_ty_at_mono_le with (k:=K); [apply Nat.le_max_l | assumption]`. But the term IH conclusion is lc_tm_at (max K (S j)) k t? ```
Lemma subst_ty_env_agree : forall sub1 sub2 T,
  (forall X, In X (ty_fv T) -> sub1 X = sub2 X) ->
  subst_ty_env sub1 T = subst_ty_env sub2 T.
Proof.
  intros sub1 sub2 T. revert sub1 sub2. induction T; intros sub1 sub2 Hagree;
    cbn [subst_ty_env ty_fv].
  - reflexivity.
  - apply Hagree. simpl. auto.
  - assert (E1 : subst_ty_env sub1 T1 = subst_ty_env sub2 T1).
    { apply IHT1. intros X Hin. apply Hagree. apply in_or_app. left. exact Hin. }
    assert (E2 : subst_ty_env sub1 T2 = subst_ty_env sub2 T2).
    { apply IHT2. intros X Hin. apply Hagree. apply in_or_app. right. exact Hin. }
    rewrite E1, E2. reflexivity.
  - assert (E : subst_ty_env sub1 T = subst_ty_env sub2 T).
    { apply IHT. intros X Hin. apply Hagree. exact Hin. }
    rewrite E. reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Let's rewrite subst_tm_env_agree:
```
Lemma subst_tm_env_agree : forall sigma1 sigma2 t,
  (forall x, In x (tm_fv t) -> sigma1 x = sigma2 x) ->
  subst_tm_env sigma1 t = subst_tm_env sigma2 t.
Proof.
  intros sigma1 sigma2 t. revert sigma1 sigma2.
  induction t as [n|x|T b IHb|a b IHa IHb|b IHb|b T IHb|c d IHc IHd];
    intros sigma1 sigma2 Hagree; cbn [subst_tm_env tm_fv].
  - reflexivity.
  - apply Hagree. simpl. auto.
  - assert (E : subst_tm_env sigma1 b = subst_tm_env sigma2 b).
    { apply IHb. intros y Hin. apply Hagree. exact Hin. }
    rewrite E. reflexivity.
  - assert (E1 : subst_tm_env sigma1 a = subst_tm_env sigma2 a).
    { apply IHa. intros y Hin. apply Hagree. apply in_or_app. left. exact Hin. }
    assert (E2 : subst_tm_env sigma1 b = subst_tm_env sigma2 b).
    { apply IHb. intros y Hin. apply Hagree. apply in_or_app. right. exact Hin. }
    rewrite E1, E2. reflexivity.
  - assert (E : subst_tm_env sigma1 b = subst_tm_env sigma2 b).
    { apply IHb. intros y Hin. apply Hagree. exact Hin. }
    rewrite E. reflexivity.
  - assert (E : subst_tm_env sigma1 b = subst_tm_env sigma2 b).
    { apply IHb. intros y Hin. apply Hagree. exact Hin. }
    rewrite E. reflexivity.
  - assert (E1 : subst_tm_env sigma1 c = subst_tm_env sigma2 c).
    { apply IHc. intros y Hin. apply Hagree. apply in_or_app. left. exact Hin. }
    assert (E2 : subst_tm_env sigma1 d = subst_tm_env sigma2 d).
    { apply IHd. intros y Hin. apply Hagree. apply in_or_app. right. exact Hin. }
    rewrite E1, E2. reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Note the tm_tapp case: subst_tm_env leaves T unchanged, so only b. ✓.

### 2. RelSem lemmas

- `RelSem_multi_l`:
```
Lemma RelSem_multi_l : forall T envB envF t1 t1' t2,
  multi t1 t1' -> RelSem T envB envF t1' t2 -> RelSem T envB envF t1 t2.
Proof.
  intros T envB envF t1 t1' t2 Hm Hrel.
  destruct T; cbn [RelSem Sem] in *.
  - (* BVar i *) destruct Hrel as (v1 & v2 & Hv1 & Hv2 & M1 & M2 & HS).
    exists v1, v2. repeat split; try assumption.
    eapply multi_trans; eassumption.
  ...
```
Hmm, `cbn [RelSem Sem]` with the `if mode` needs mode = true; RelSem is defined as `Sem true T envB envF`, so cbn should reduce `Sem true (BVar i)` — but Sem is a Fixpoint whose first match is on T, so `cbn [RelSem Sem]` might unfold and reduce the if true. Let's test. In each case, the shape: exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ REST. The goal after exists v1,v2: need value, value, multi t1 v1 (from Hm: multi t1 t1' and M1: multi t1' v1), multi t2 v2 (unchanged), REST. Use `repeat split; try assumption; eapply multi_trans; eassumption.` But `repeat split` will also split the REST part if it's a conjunction? The REST for Arrow is `value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall...`; repeat split would break the forall? It might split too much, generating weird goals. Better destruct and reconstruct explicitly per case:
```
  - destruct Hrel as (v1 & v2 & Hv1 & Hv2 & M1 & M2 & Hrest).
    exists v1, v2. repeat split.
    + exact Hv1. + exact Hv2.
    + eapply multi_trans; eassumption.
    + exact M2. + exact Hrest.
```
`repeat split` on goal `value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ REST` produces 5 goals where the last is REST itself? Since /\ is right-associative: value v1 /\ (value v2 /\ (multi ... /\ (multi ... /\ REST))). repeat split: splits outer then recursively; the last component REST is not a conjunction (for Arrow, REST = value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall... which IS a conjunction!). Hmm, `repeat split` would split REST too, but we want to use Hrest directly. Instead use `split` three times and then exact:
```
    exists v1, v2. split; [exact Hv1|]. split; [exact Hv2|]. split.
    + eapply multi_trans; eassumption.
    + split; [exact M2| exact Hrest].
```
That's verbose. Alternative: define a helper `RelSem_unfold` predicate? Or use `repeat split; try assumption` and hope REST is closed by assumption at the end? For Arrow, REST = value v1 /\ value v2 /\ forall...; after repeat split, goals include value v1 (duplicate, assumption), value v2, and the forall (assumption can find Hrest if Hrest has the whole conjunction? No, after splitting, the forall goal can't be closed by Hrest (a conjunction), but Hrest's second projection... assumption won't project). So no.

Maybe prove a generic lemma with a uniform shape by generalizing the "body" formula. Define for each type T the body B(T) such that RelSem T env t1 t2 = ∃ v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧ Body(T) v1 v2. Since Sem's true branch is uniform, we can write a lemma:

```
Lemma RelSem_intro : forall T envB envF t1 t2 v1 v2,
  value v1 -> value v2 -> multi t1 v1 -> multi t2 v2 ->
  ValSem T envB envF v1 v2 ->
  RelSem T envB envF t1 t2.
```
Wait, is `RelSem T t1 t2` equivalent to `∃ v1 v2 values multis ∧ ValSem T v1 v2`? The true branch's inner formula is not literally ValSem T v1 v2 for Arrow? Let's check: Arrow true inner = value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ forall... = ValSem Arrow v1 v2 ✓ (by definition). All true inner = value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ forall... = ValSem All v1 v2 ✓. BVar/FVar inner = nth_rel/envF = ValSem ✓. So yes! The true branch is uniformly `∃ v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧ ValSem T v1 v2`. Great. Claim:

```
Lemma RelSem_def : forall T envB envF t1 t2,
  RelSem T envB envF t1 t2 <->
  exists v1 v2, value v1 /\ value v2 /\ multi t1 v1 /\ multi t2 v2 /\ ValSem T envB envF v1 v2.
```
Proof: destruct T; cbn [RelSem ValSem Sem]; tauto-ish. Actually for Arrow, after cbn, RelSem's inner is value v1 /\ value v2 /\ (forall...) and ValSem is value t1 /\ value t2 /\ forall...; with t1=v1,t2=v2 they're syntactically the same. So `destruct T; cbn; split; intros H; [destruct H as (...)|]; ...` might work with `firstorder` or manual. hmm, `cbn` may not fully reduce the `if true`. Let's test.

If RelSem_def holds, then:
- RelSem_multi_l: Hrel gives witnesses; RelSem_intro with composed multi.
- RelSem_multi_r similarly.
- RelSem_of_val: if ValSem T v1 v2 and value v1 v2, then RelSem via intro with multi_refl.
- ValSem to RelSem for values.

Also `RelSem_multi_l` can be proven as:
```
intros T envB envF t1 t1' t2 Hm (v1 & v2 & Hv1 & Hv2 & M1 & M2 & HV). 
```
Using RelSem_def as an iff: rewrite RelSem_def in Hrel; destruct Hrel; ...; apply RelSem_def; exists...

Let's test whether `RelSem_def` is provable by `destruct T; cbn; split; intros [...]; eauto` with some manual forall handling. We can also define ValSem and RelSem so that this is by construction? We defined Sem with duplicated formulas; we could redefine RelSem to literally be the existential over ValSem, with ValSem still a Fixpoint using RelSem at subterms—mutual recursion issue. But with the boolean flag, the true branch duplicates; cbn should show they match.

Let's experiment in a scratch quickly: add the lemma and see. We'll write it with `destruct T; cbn [RelSem ValSem Sem]; try reflexivity?`. Actually `RelSem (Ty_BVar i) envB envF t1 t2` is `Sem true (Ty_BVar i) envB envF t1 t2`, which cbn reduces to `if true then (exists ...) else ...` and then to the exists. ValSem (BVar i) v1 v2 = `nth_rel i envB v1 v2`. The RHS of RelSem_def is ∃ v1 v2, value... ∧ nth_rel ...; exactly the LHS. So for BVar, reflexivity? `RelSem_multi_l`:

Lemma RelSem_multi_l : forall T envB envF t1 t1' t2,
  multi t1 t1' -> RelSem T envB envF t1' t2 -> RelSem T envB envF t1 t2.
Proof.
  intros T envB envF t1 t1' t2 Hm Hrel.
  apply RelSem_def in Hrel. apply RelSem_def.
  destruct Hrel as (v1 & v2 & Hv1 & Hv2 & M1 & M2 & HV).
  exists v1, v2. split; [assumption|]. split; [assumption|]. split.
  - eapply multi_trans; eassumption.
  - split; assumption.
Qed.
```

### Identity extension lemma

We need:
```
Lemma Sem_open_ty : forall T k U envB envF,
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->
  k <= length envB ->
  (forall t1 t2, RelSem (open_ty_rec k U T) envB envF t1 t2 <->
                RelSem T (set_at_rel k (ValSem U envB envF) envB) envF t1 t2) /\
  (forall t1 t2, ValSem (open_ty_rec k U T) envB envF t1 t2 <->
                ValSem T (set_at_rel k (ValSem U envB envF) envB) envF t1 t2).
```
Proof by induction on T generalizing k envB envF? envF doesn't change. The RHS's V = ValSem U envB envF depends on envB, so when we go under an All with envB' = S::envB, V' = ValSem U (S::envB) envF = ValSem U envB envF by lc U (envB independence). We need the envB independence lemma first.

Before writing, define the "pointwise relation equality" helpers:
```
Definition rel_iff (R1 R2 : tm -> tm -> Prop) := forall a b, R1 a b <-> R2 a b.
Definition envB_agree K e1 e2 := forall i, i < K -> rel_iff (nth_rel i e1) (nth_rel i e2).
```
Using rel_iff and envB_agree definitions will simplify statements. Let's add them.

nth_rel set_at lemmas:
```
Lemma nth_rel_set_at_eq : forall k R env, k <= length env ->
  rel_iff (nth_rel k (set_at_rel k R env)) R.
Proof.
  intros k R env Hlen a b. unfold nth_rel, set_at_rel.
  rewrite nth_error_app1? 
```
We can reason with nth_error and app. Let's use list lemmas: `nth_error (firstn k env ++ R :: skipn (S k) env) k`. Since k ≤ length env, firstn k env has length k (firstn_length_le). nth_error (l1 ++ l2) k with k=length l1 → nth_error l2 0 = Some R. There are lemmas: `nth_error_app2`, `nth_error_skipn`, etc. Alternatively prove by induction on env and k? Let's do direct with lemmas:
- firstn_length_le: length (firstn k env) = min k (length env) = k (if k≤len). `rewrite firstn_length_le by lia` gives length (firstn k env) = min k (length env)? The lemma `firstn_length_le : forall l n, n <= length l -> length (firstn n l) = n`. ✓.
- nth_error_app2: forall l l' n, length l <= n -> nth_error (l++l') n = nth_error l' (n - length l). So nth_error (firstn k env ++ R::...) k = nth_error (R::...) (k - length (firstn k env)) = nth_error (R::...) 0 = Some R. ✓.
Prove rel_iff by simplification: unfold nth_rel, set_at_rel; rewrite nth_error_app2; rewrite firstn_length_le; replace (k-k) with 0 by lia; reflexivity. The `if` is inside the definition; after nth_error gives Some R, the match reduces. Let's write:
```
Lemma nth_rel_set_at_eq : forall k R env, k <= length env ->
  rel_iff (nth_rel k (set_at_rel k R env)) R.
Proof.
  intros k R env Hlen a b. unfold nth_rel, set_at_rel.
  rewrite nth_error_app2 by (rewrite firstn_length_le; lia).
  replace (k - length (firstn k env)) with 0 by (rewrite firstn_length_le; lia).
  reflexivity.
Qed.
```
`rewrite nth_error_app2` may need the side condition `length (firstn k env) <= k`; our by clause proves it. Then nth_error (R :: skipn...) 0 = Some R; reflexivity.

- nth_rel_set_at_lt:
```
Lemma nth_rel_set_at_lt : forall i k R env, i < k -> k <= length env ->
  rel_iff (nth_rel i (set_at_rel k R env)) (nth_rel i env).
Proof.
  intros i k R env Hlt Hlen a b. unfold nth_rel, set_at_rel.
  assert (Hlenk : length (firstn k env) = k) by (rewrite firstn_length_le; lia).
  rewrite nth_error_app1 by (rewrite Hlenk; lia).
  rewrite Hlenk. rewrite <- nth_error_app1? 
```

  rewrite (firstn_skipn k env) at 2.  (* replace env by firstn k env ++ skipn k env *)

Line 1244: RelSem_congr_gen proof's last tactic line:
```
    split; [exact H3|]; split; [exact H4|];
    [apply HV | apply HV]; assumption.
```
The bracketed `[apply HV | apply HV]` expects 2 goals but there are maybe different. Let's inspect the goal structure after applying RelSem_def. RelSem_def: ∃ v1 v2, value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ multi t1 v1 ∧ multi t2 v2 ∧ ValSem. After `exists v1,v2`, the goal is a conjunction of 5. Our split sequence: split→2 goals (value v1, rest). First done. split→ (value v2, rest). done. split→ (multi t1 v1, rest). H3 done. split→ (multi t2 v2, ValSem). H4 done; remaining ValSem. So after the fourth split, only ONE goal remains. The syntax `split; [exact H3|]; split; [exact H4|]; [apply HV | apply HV]` has the last bracket with 2 tactics but only 1 goal. Just use `apply HV; assumption`.

Patch.

  + intros (v1 & v2 & H1 & H2 & H3 & H4 & H5). exists v1,v2.
    split; [exact H1|].
    split; [exact H2|].
    split; [exact H3|].
    split; [exact H4|].
    apply HV; assumption.
  + intros (...). ...
```
Actually `split; [exact H1|]` — the `|` with empty second branch: is that allowed? It means focus first goal with exact H1, and the remaining goals are handled by the rest. The trailing semicolon before the next split might apply split to *both* the first goal (already closed?) and the rest, causing count mismatch. Let's avoid semicolons:
```
  split.
  - intros (v1 & v2 & H1 & H2 & H3 & H4 & H5). exists v1, v2.
    split. exact H1.
    split. exact H2.
    split. exact H3.
    split. exact H4.
    apply HV. assumption.
  - intros (...). exists...; same.
```

  lc_ty_at 0 U -> k <= length envB -> lc_ty_at (S k) T ->
  forall v1 v2,

has_type_lc_ty compiles! Now the fundamental theorem. This is the biggest piece. Let's carefully write it.

Notation/definitions used:
- `subst_ty_env sub T` for types.
- `subst_tm_ty_env sub t` for terms.
- `subst_tm_env sigma t`.
- `update_sub sub X A`, `update_tyenv envF X S`, `update_tmenv sigma x a`.

Fundamental theorem statement:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall (sub : atom -> ty) (envF : TyRelEnv) (sigma1 sigma2 : atom -> tm),
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (sub X)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (sigma1 x)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (sigma2 x)) ->
    (forall x U, lookup_context x Gamma = Some U -> RelSem U [] envF (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x)) ->
    RelSem T [] envF (subst_tm_env sigma1 (subst_tm_ty_env sub t))
                      (subst_tm_env sigma2 (subst_tm_ty_env sub t)).
```
Proof by induction on H, generalizing sub envF sigma1 sigma2 and the hypotheses? We can do `intros Delta Gamma t T H. induction H; intros sub envF sigma1 sigma2 Hsub Hlc1 Hlc2 Henv.`

Case T_Var:
- goal: RelSem T [] envF (subst_tm_env sigma1 (subst_tm_ty_env sub (tm_fvar x))) (subst_tm_env sigma2 ...) = RelSem T [] envF (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x) (since subst_tm_ty_env on tm_fvar x = tm_fvar x, then subst_tm gives sigma x). Henv x T H gives exactly that (lookup_context x Gamma = Some T). 
```
  - cbn [subst_tm_env subst_tm_ty_env]. apply (Henv x T H).
```
Note the constructor T_Var fields: H : lookup_context x Gamma = Some T, H0 : wf_ty Delta T. The case's goal type is T. `apply (Henv x T H)`.

Case T_Abs:

From has_type_lc, T_Abs's IH was H1 (forall x, ~In x L -> lc...). For the fundamental theorem, the IH will be `H1 : forall x, ~In x L -> (forall sub envF sigma1 sigma2, ... -> RelSem T2 [] envF (subst ... (open_tm t2 (fvar x))) ...)`. Hmm, the trailing foralls are part of the IH if the motive includes them. We'll use it as such.

The term after substitution: because subst_tm_ty_env sub (tm_abs T1 t2) = tm_abs (subst_ty_env sub T1) (subst_tm_ty_env sub t2), and subst_tm_env distributes: tm_abs (subst_ty_env sub T1) (subst_tm_env sigma_i (subst_tm_ty_env sub t2)) = tm_abs (subst_ty_env sub T1) body_i.

Case T_App:
Fields: T1,T2,t1,t2, H : has_type t1 (Arrow T1 T2), H0 : has_type t2 T1, IHhas_type1 : forall sub envF sigma1 sigma2, ... -> RelSem (Arrow T1 T2) [] envF (...t1...), IHhas_type2 : ... -> RelSem T1 [] envF (...t2...).

Writing the fundamental theorem:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Delta Gamma t T,
  has_type Delta Gamma t T ->
  forall sub envF sigma1 sigma2,
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (sub X)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (sigma1 x)) ->
    (forall x, lc_tm_at 0 0 (sigma2 x)) ->
    (forall x U, lookup_context x Gamma = Some U ->
       RelSem U [] envF (sigma1 x) (sigma2 x)) ->
    RelSem T [] envF (subst_tm_env sigma1 (subst_tm_ty_env sub t))
                     (subst_tm_env sigma2 (subst_tm_ty_env sub t)).
Proof.
  intros Delta Gamma t T H.
  induction H; intros sub envF sigma1 sigma2 Hsub Hlc1 Hlc2 Henv.
  - (* T_Var *) cbn [subst_tm_env subst_tm_ty_env]. apply (Henv x T H).
  ...
```

Hmm, in the T_Var case, the field names: T_Var : forall Delta Gamma x T, lookup_context x Gamma = Some T -> wf_ty Delta T -> has_type ... (tm_fvar x) T. After induction, the hypothesis names for the two premises: H and H0? Since H is consumed by induction, the premises become H and H0 likely. Our `intros ... H; induction H` may name them H and H0. We refer to the lookup premise as H. Let's check: In the T_Var case, the goal is RelSem T [] ...; Henv needs lookup. If the premise name is H, good. But maybe the induction renames it to H0 because the original H is replaced? Standard: `induction H` in a context where H is a hypothesis of an inductive type: the case's hypotheses are named H, H0, ... in order. So T_Var: H : lookup..., H0 : wf_ty...; goal. Let's try.

```
Lemma notin_app_l : forall (A : Type) (l1 l2 : list A) (x : A),
  ~ In x (l1 ++ l2) -> ~ In x l1.
Proof. intros A l1 l2 x H Hc. apply H. apply in_or_app. left. exact Hc. Qed.
Lemma notin_app_r : forall (A : Type) (l1 l2 : list A) (x : A),
  ~ In x (l1 ++ l2) -> ~ In x l2.
Proof. intros A l1 l2 x H Hc. apply H. apply in_or_app. right. exact Hc. Qed.
```
